Went through two review rounds: v1 7.8/10 (6 corrections: fix a genuine Day 1 all-B-path bug where the nourishment break silently added a second egg on top of breakfast's egg, directly contradicting the draft's own verification-note claim that this had been checked; remove Brazil nuts from the actual meal chart entirely — two nuts alone can exceed the pregnancy reference amount, and they aren't an ordinary/affordable Indian household food — keeping them only as an educational caution, plus a tagline change since "a nut or two" read as a mild recommendation; present both the US 400mcg and the newer EFSA 255mcg upper limits honestly rather than only the US figure; correct "selenium acts as an antioxidant" to the more precise "selenium is part of proteins that help protect cells from oxidative damage," and "helps convert thyroid hormone" to "selenium-containing enzymes help activate and deactivate thyroid hormones"; soften an overconfident vegetarian-adequacy claim; diversify a repeated "vegetarian breakfast + boiled egg alongside" pattern that had appeared on all six active days, and reduce curd's over-frequent appearance) → v2 9.0/10 (a second review round catching that Claude's own v2 changelog didn't match the actual document in two places — claimed the boiled-egg pattern was cut to two days but it still appeared on three, and claimed curd was reduced to three occurrences but same-day repetition remained on Days 3 and 6 — both fixed for real this time, plus tightening an ambiguous supplement-caution sentence and softening "most concentrated common food source" since Brazil nuts aren't common in an Indian context) → locked directly off the "I would be comfortable locking Week 30" verdict, per the standing rule. Saved as `Nourish - Third Trimester Week 30 (LOCKED).docx`. Week 30's own "Looking ahead" line names **vitamin K** as next — Claude's best guess, not yet verified against the full locked-week theme list; confirm before treating it as settled, per the standing rule below.

**Week 30 lessons, worth carrying into every future week:** (1) a "not yet verified against the full ICMR-NIN table set" nutrient is worth checking against the *full* RDA/EAR report, not just the shorter summary PDF used repeatedly since Week 23 — selenium turned out to have a real, confirmed ICMR-NIN figure that the shorter document simply doesn't include, sitting in a separate "other minerals and trace elements" table alongside phosphorus/sodium/potassium/copper/manganese/chromium; (2) when a changelog claims a specific fix ("reduced to two days," "reduced to three occurrences"), that claim must be verified against the actual final document by literally re-counting, not assumed true because the edit was made somewhere in the file — this project's own verification note has now been caught overstating a fix's completeness at least three times (Week 28, Week 29, and now Week 30 twice in one round), meaning this specific failure mode recurs and needs an actual counting/tracing step, not just a description, before submitting each version; (3) a genuinely concentrated single-food source (Brazil nuts here) is safer taught as an educational caution than as a literal selectable meal-chart option, especially when the food isn't an ordinary household item in the target country — the fact can still be taught accurately without building a menu choice around it; (4) when two legitimate authorities set different numeric safety ceilings for the same nutrient (US 400mcg vs EFSA 255mcg for selenium, matching the WHO-vs-ICMR pattern from Week 27), present both honestly rather than picking one silently, and frame neither as a target; (5) a supplement-caution sentence combining three conditions with "and" can be misread as applying only when all three co-occur — safety-critical sentences need a structure that can't be parsed as conditional when it's meant to be absolute; (6) describing an imported/specialty food as "common" is its own small but real accuracy issue distinct from the nutritional facts about it — accuracy applies to framing words, not just numbers; (7) a real, verifiable condition-specific note is worth including when the evidence genuinely supports it (the ATA's actual recommendation against selenium supplementation for TPOAb-positive pregnant women) — this project's standing restraint against manufacturing condition notes is about not inventing unverified links, not about avoiding condition notes altogether.

**Week 31 (vitamin K) — LOCKED and LIVE, 2026-08-05, same session as Week 30.** Topic verified clear of duplication against the full list of 30 locked-week themes before drafting (per the standing rule) — vitamin K had never appeared. ICMR-NIN 2020's full RDA/EAR report (already fetched for Week 30, re-checked here) states, in prose rather than a table: "The recommendation for vitamin K is 55μg for adults and is in tune with recommendations of FAO/WHO" — a single general-adult figure, no pregnancy value, no gender split — presented honestly alongside NIH's pregnancy Adequate Intake (90 mcg/day for adults, 75 mcg/day for ages 14-18). Vitamin K has no established upper limit at all (a genuine structural departure from the last several weeks' "more isn't the goal" framing), so the week's real safety story became the newborn vitamin K injection and warfarin, not overconsumption. Went through two review rounds: v1 7.7/10 (9 corrections: soften an absolute newborn-vitamin-K sentence — "this has nothing to do with how much you ate" — since ordinary maternal intake doesn't affect the newborn's naturally low stores but isn't literally irrelevant either; narrow "blood thinner" to specifically warfarin and similar vitamin-K antagonists, since not every anticoagulant interacts the same way, and add that warfarin isn't usually used during pregnancy except specialist situations plus an explicit "never stop or change medicine yourself" line; qualify the "no upper limit" reassurance so it can't read as license to supplement freely, especially given the warfarin interaction; reduce a repeated "vegetarian breakfast + boiled egg alongside" construction that had returned across all six active days despite being corrected the week before; convert several additive B options — a full chicken dish served beside an already-complete paneer or dal preparation — into genuine substitutions where the non-vegetarian choice replaces the main protein; reduce spinach from appearing on five of six active days down to three, using cabbage, green beans, methi, and broccoli instead; broaden the food-source language beyond spinach to include broccoli, cabbage, and vegetable oils; simplify a tagline that was trying to carry both the food message and the newborn-injection message in one line) → v2 9.2/10 (3 final corrections: the spinach-reduction claim was itself inaccurate — two more spinach occurrences, in Day 2's paratha and Day 5's cheela, had been missed and needed real substitutes, cabbage in both cases; Day 4's full all-B path contained chicken three times across breakfast/lunch/dinner despite varied preparations, fixed by changing lunch B to a fully cooked egg curry; Day 5's dinner B dropped the vegetable component entirely when swapping to chicken tikka, fixed by adding a shared vegetable-sabzi side) → locked directly off the "I would lock Week 31" verdict, per the standing rule. Saved as `Nourish - Third Trimester Week 31 (LOCKED).docx`. Week 31's own "Looking ahead" line names **vitamin B6** as next — Claude's best guess, not yet verified against the full locked-week theme list; confirm before treating it as settled, per the standing rule below.

**Week 31 lessons, worth carrying into every future week:** (1) a nutrient's own real story can require deviating from the recent "more isn't the goal" template shape — vitamin K genuinely has no established upper limit, so forcing an overconsumption-caution day onto it would have been dishonest; the actual safety story (newborn injection, warfarin) needed its own two dedicated days instead; (2) an absolute-sounding safety sentence can overcorrect into a different inaccuracy — "this has nothing to do with how much you ate" swung too far from a genuine claim (poor placental transfer, not maternal diet, causes newborn vitamin K deficiency) into an overstated one; precision sometimes means adding back a qualifier ("even when their mother ate a healthy diet") rather than just asserting the mechanism; (3) a broad safety category ("blood thinner") can be less accurate than the specific drug class actually meant (warfarin/vitamin K antagonists) — different anticoagulants can have entirely different interaction profiles, and naming the wrong scope either over-restricts mothers on unrelated medications or under-warns the ones who need it; (4) a corrected pattern from the immediately preceding week (Week 30's "boiled egg alongside every breakfast") can silently return in the very next week's v1 if each week is drafted somewhat fresh — the fix needs to become a standing habit checked at draft time, not just something corrected reactively per-week after review; (5) "the B option should replace the featured food, not add a second complete dish beside it" (established narrowly for nourishment-break pairs at Week 22) generalizes to full lunch/dinner mains too — a non-vegetarian choice that keeps an entire vegetarian dish as a "side" produces an unrealistically heavy meal, not a real alternative; (6) a single vegetable can quietly dominate a week even after an explicit reduction pass — spinach was undercounted by two real occurrences because paratha and cheela fillings weren't checked as carefully as named "sabzi" sides; every ingredient appearing inside a stuffed/mixed dish needs the same tally discipline as one named as a standalone side; (7) auditing a day's full all-B path for protein repetition must count by broad category (chicken vs. chicken vs. chicken), not just by whether each individual preparation has a different name — three differently-prepared chicken dishes in one day is still three chicken meals; (8) when a B option swaps the protein source, the accompanying vegetable side must be re-checked too, not assumed to carry over automatically — Day 5's fix in v1 dropped its vegetable component entirely while fixing the protein, a new gap introduced by the same edit that fixed the original one.

**The standing per-week workflow, used for every week and to be used for Week 23 onward:**
1. I infer the week's nutrient topic from the previous locked week's "Looking ahead" closing line (Week 22's locked file ends with: "Next week, we'll look at omega-3 fats — another nutrient with a direct role in your baby's brain and eye development.") — I do NOT wait to be told the topic.
2. I research the topic via WebSearch: the real ICMR-NIN 2020 figure for that nutrient in pregnancy if one genuinely exists (never invented or borrowed from a different body's guidance — if ICMR-NIN has no figure, as with fiber and choline, say so honestly and use a clearly-labeled international reference instead, e.g. NIH/WHO/ACOG), core physiological functions from a primary source (NIH ODS fact sheets are the reliable default), India-specific food/cultural context, and any genuine safety/interaction nuance.
3. I draft a full v1 as a `.docx` via a Node `docx`-package build script (see "Technical setup"), save it to the outputs folder, and present it to Roop for review — never to the locked mom-village folder at this stage.
4. Roop reviews in detail against her own external sources, replies with specific numbered corrections and a score out of 10, and either says to send a v2 or gives a "close to lockable" verdict with a small number of final changes.
5. I apply corrections precisely — targeted fixes, not wholesale rewrites — and produce v2 (occasionally v3) with a "Changes from vN" changelog (bulleted, marked "not mother-facing") and an updated "Verification basis" paragraph (also "not mother-facing") documenting exactly what was corrected and why, both placed after the draft-label line and before "Weekly nourishment theme." I present each new version for review the same way.
6. Once Roop gives an explicit lock signal (the word "lock," or a verdict like "should be ready to lock" following a final small set of corrections), I build the locked version: strip the "DRAFT vN — for review, not locked" line entirely (replaced with the week's short mantra/tagline as an italic subtitle — e.g. Week 22's became "A building block for your baby's brain"), strip the entire changelog + verification-basis block, rename the output file from "(DRAFT vN)" to "(LOCKED)", run the build, verify the file was actually written via `ls` (a filename-substitution bug bit me once on Week 16 — always verify, don't assume), copy it into the real `/Users/roop/Downloads/mom-village/` folder, and present it via `mcp__cowork__present_files`. I do not skip presenting/confirming just because a verdict sounds final — I complete the actual copy+present steps before considering a week done.
7. If Roop types the next week's number before I've gotten an explicit lock signal on the current week, I pause and ask her to confirm the lock rather than assuming — this happened once (Week 19→20) and was well-received.

**Standing content rules, reinforced repeatedly across weeks and still in force for Week 23 onward:**
- Never manufacture a health-condition note (thyroid/PCOS/diabetes_gd/high_bp) unless genuinely, verifiably relevant to that week's nutrient — a growing number of recent weeks (Vitamin C, Zinc, Choline) have deliberately ended with **zero** condition-specific notes after a claimed connection couldn't be verified strongly enough (e.g. Choline's animal-model-heavy fatty-liver/GDM link was investigated and explicitly left out). This restraint has been explicitly praised by Roop as evidence the principle is working, not a content gap — don't feel pressure to force a condition note into every week.
- Every clinical/nutritional claim must be independently verified; ICMR-NIN-attributed figures must be genuinely ICMR-NIN, never conflated with WHO/ACOG/NHS/NIH figures. When ICMR-NIN has no figure for a nutrient at all (confirmed for fiber and for choline), say so honestly and use a clearly-labeled international reference instead of inventing or borrowing one.
- Don't overclaim evidence — mixed, small, single-trial, or population-specific effects (e.g. choline's 930mg-vs-480mg RCT finding on infant attention, set against a broader systematic review finding mixed results) must be stated with real uncertainty, held in tension rather than resolved one way.
- Don't overclaim mechanism-to-outcome either — e.g. Week 22 v1's "supports your baby's brain and memory development" was corrected (Week 22 v2 review) to the more measured "supports normal brain and nervous-system development and helps form cell membranes and acetylcholine, an important chemical messenger," matching NIH's own phrasing rather than implying a settled, direct cognitive outcome.
- Be precise about biological timing — Week 22 v2's review caught that neural-tube closure happens very early in pregnancy, not during Second Trimester Week 22 itself; the "why it matters now" framing was corrected to make clear that's something that happened earlier, not something currently in progress. Watch for this class of error (stating a fetal-development fact as "happening now" when it actually happened in an earlier trimester) in every future week.
- Don't name specific local species/fish as verified-safe/verified-risky (e.g. "rohu, katla, Indian mackerel/bangda") unless the cited source actually classifies those specific species — Week 22 v2's review caught that the FDA/EPA source used doesn't classify Indian freshwater fish by name; corrected to generic "choose fish known to be lower in mercury, vary the type, and follow reliable local guidance where available," with sardines named only because they're genuinely and specifically covered by that guidance. Apply the same specificity discipline before naming any other India-specific food item as clinically verified.
- Fish capped at roughly two to three meals per week combined across all daily choices, stated explicitly in the "If you eat meat, fish, or eggs" intro box, alongside a general "vary the type, cook fully" instruction rather than a specific list of named safe species unless a source actually supports naming them.
- Non-vegetarian meal alternatives must never quietly drop the week's featured nutrient; vegetarian alternatives need real protein/nutrient parity with their non-veg counterpart, not a downgrade.
- Vegetarian-adequacy reassurance must be honest, not falsely comforting — avoid vague phrasing like "meaningful intake" that implies near-automatic adequacy; state plainly when plant sources provide genuinely smaller per-serving amounts than animal sources and that deliberate combining across the day is what makes the difference (this exact correction was needed on Choline's Week 22 v1→v2 pass and is likely to recur on any future animal-concentrated nutrient).
- A/B meal alternatives (especially nourishment-break pairs) must be genuinely comparable in the target nutrient and satiety, not "alternatives in name only" — Week 22 v1 had four mismatched nourishment-break pairs (e.g. an egg vs. plain milk) flagged and fixed into real combinations in v2. Check every A/B pair for this before presenting a draft.
- When a day is framed around one dietary path (e.g. "for vegetarian mothers" / "building choline without meat or fish"), make sure the day's B option doesn't contradict that framing (Week 22 v1's "For vegetarian mothers" day offered a chicken option, which read as contradictory) — either keep B within the same dietary spirit, or add an explicit one-line disambiguator like "Choose A for an egg-free meal or B if you include eggs" so the mother isn't confused about which path applies to her.
- Meal variety must be actively managed across a week — avoid overusing "chicken curry"/"boiled egg"/the same 2-3 vegetables (cauliflower/cabbage/rajma) across every day; rotate formats (keema, stew, pulao, grilled, bhurji, omelette, roti-roll) and rotate vegetables/legumes deliberately. This has needed a correction almost every week and should be self-checked before presenting v1, not left for Roop to catch.
- Avoid anxiety-provoking, causal-sounding, or coercive language about pregnancy symptoms/complications; when a safety point can be made without naming a specific frightening complication, prefer the calmer route (established explicitly on Week 21's zinc/preterm-birth discussion, which was removed entirely in favor of calm supplement-caution language).
- Day 7 stays a free-choice "Reflection" day with no forced A/B choices.
- The "If you eat meat, fish, or eggs" framing must accurately reflect that specific nutrient's real animal-vs-plant distribution — get the direction and degree right per nutrient (plant-exclusive like fiber/vitamin C vs. animal-concentrated like vitamin D/zinc/choline vs. roughly diet-neutral like iodine/hydration), never a copy-pasted template.
- A recurring "more isn't the goal" throughline applies to any nutrient with a supplement form: don't imply higher doses are better, direct mothers to their maternity team before adding a supplement beyond food + their existing prenatal, don't assert country-specific claims (e.g. "many Indian prenatal supplements lack X") unless the cited source actually covers the Indian market — Choline's Week 22 v1 made exactly this mistake (the evidence was US-market-only) and was corrected to neutral "not every prenatal supplement contains this — check your label" phrasing.
- Salad/raw-produce safety language stays standardized ("a thoroughly washed, freshly prepared salad") and appropriately cautious about away-from-home settings specifically.
- Locked files must never retain the "Changes from vN" changelog or "Verification basis" paragraph — stripped at lock time, which also resolves draft-stage pagination artifacts (stranded trailing content, uneven page balance). Don't assume this resolves perfectly without actually re-rendering and checking the locked file, per Roop's explicit note on Week 22's final review.

**Technical setup, to be recreated in a fresh session (this exact toolchain does not persist):** Node's `docx` npm package, used via a per-week build script following this exact shared-helper pattern (reconstruct verbatim — this is the proven, Roop-approved visual template, not a starting point to redesign):
- Colors: `INDIGO = "1F2E4D"` (headings/titles), `SAGE = "5F7052"` (body/notes/labels), `TERRA = "BE6240"` (condition notes, safety notes, "If you eat meat, fish, or eggs" callouts), gold `"A97418"` as a top border rule on each day heading.
- Helper functions: `h(text, level)` for headings, `p(text, {italics, bold, color, after})` for styled paragraphs, `bullet(text)`, `labelLine(label, text, color)` (returns null if text is falsy — used to conditionally render optional lines), `choiceBlock(label, a, b)` (renders "Label — choose one: A. ... B. ..." or a single `labelLine` if b is falsy), `dayHeading(dayNum, role)` (bold INDIGO text, gold top border, `keepNext: true` so it never orphans from its content), `dayCard(dayNum, role, d)` (assembles one full day from a day-object).
- Day-object shape: `{ role, today, afterToday (optional — a short disambiguator line rendered right after "Today" and before the meal choices, added for Week 22's vegetarian-day clarity fix and worth reusing whenever a day needs one), breakfastA/B, lunchA/B, nbreakA/B, dinnerA/B, stillHungry, easier (rarely used), note (italic SAGE, general commentary), safetyNote (TERRA "Worth knowing" line, for general non-condition-gated cautions), conditionFlag/conditionNote (TERRA "If this applies to you (X):" line, only when a real verified condition-specific claim exists), source (rarely used, grey italic) }`.
- Document structure top to bottom: title (`NOURISH — [Trimester], Week N`, bold INDIGO size 32) + subtitle line (draft: "DRAFT vN — for review, not locked" italic SAGE; locked: the week's short mantra/tagline, italic SAGE) → (draft only) "Changes from vN" bulleted changelog + "Verification basis" paragraph, both TERRA-labeled "(not mother-facing)" → "Weekly nourishment theme" (H1) with the theme name (bold INDIGO) and an italic SAGE mantra quote → "Why it matters now" (H2) paragraph covering function + RDA/AI framing → "If you eat meat, fish, or eggs" TERRA callout → seven `dayCard` blocks (Day 1 "Anchor" through Day 7 "Reflection," with 5 flexible thematic days in between — reuse role-name patterns like "Why it matters now," "Where [nutrient] naturally shows up," a vegetarian-focused day, "Away from home," and a supplement-caution/"more isn't the goal" day, but don't force identical role names/order onto every week if the nutrient's actual story suggests a different shape) → "Gentle weekly reflection" (H2) question → "Looking ahead" (H2) one-line teaser naming next week's topic → "Food safety basics" (H2), a **fixed, identical-every-week** 9-bullet list (cook eggs/meat/fish through; cook sprouts, never raw; pasteurized dairy; wash produce; safe water/boil-or-purify/no unknown ice; caffeine under 200mg/day; no alcohol; refrigerate promptly + reheat until steaming; lower-mercury fish, cooked fully) — do not vary this list's wording week to week even if a week's own fish guidance gets more specific elsewhere in that week's content (Roop's Week 22 review asked for more specific fish guidance, which was correctly added to the nutrient-specific "If you eat meat, fish, or eggs" callout, not to this fixed universal footer).
- Page setup: `new Document({ sections: [{ properties: { page: { size: { width: 12240, height: 15840 } } }, children }] })` (US Letter), built via `Packer.toBuffer(doc).then(buf => fs.writeFileSync(path, buf))`.
- File-naming convention: `Nourish - [First/Second/Third] Trimester Week N (DRAFT v1/v2/...).docx` for drafts (saved to the session outputs folder and presented from there), `Nourish - [Trimester] Week N (LOCKED).docx` for the final version (saved directly into `/Users/roop/Downloads/mom-village/` and presented from there). **Trimester boundaries, confirmed against ACOG's standard definition at Week 28 (see the Week 28 entry above for the full correction story): Weeks 1-13 = First Trimester, Weeks 14-27 = Second Trimester, Weeks 28+ = Third Trimester.** This was previously logged as open-ended ("Weeks 14+ = Second Trimester") and had drifted past its real end point by Week 28 — now closed and settled.

**Week 32 (vitamin B6) — LOCKED and LIVE, 2026-08-05, same session as Week 31.** Topic verified clear of duplication against the full list of 31 locked-week themes before drafting (per the standing rule) — vitamin B6 had never appeared. A genuine, confirmed ICMR-NIN 2020 pregnancy-specific figure exists this time — unlike vitamin E and vitamin K — sitting directly in the full RDA/EAR report's primary RDA table (not just prose): EAR 1.9 mg/day, RDA 2.3 mg/day for pregnant women, versus 1.9 mg/day for a general adult woman, presented honestly alongside NIH's close-but-not-identical US pregnancy RDA of 1.9 mg/day. Went through two review rounds: v1 7.1/10 ("do not lock v1" — the central issue was that Day 3's morning-sickness framing read as generic first-trimester advice rather than genuinely adapted to Week 32/third-trimester timing; 11 corrections: reframe Day 3 specifically around third-trimester nausea, renaming it "If nausea has continued or returned"; add ACOG-aligned pre-eclampsia warning signs — persistent headache, vision changes, pain under the ribs or upper abdomen, sudden swelling, breathlessness, feeling markedly unwell — since late-pregnancy nausea can signal something needing assessment rather than just B6; add NHS-aligned severe-vomiting/dehydration guidance as its own safety note; remove the exact self-dosing schedule (10-25mg 3-4x/day) from mother-facing text, since professional guidance shouldn't be presented as a self-dosing instruction inside a meal plan, especially when the total sits close to EFSA's much lower 12mg/day limit; add an explicit note that a prenatal, a B-complex product, and a separate nausea treatment can stack; correct Day 4's toxicity wording, which had reassuringly implied the risk only came from "very high doses over months to years" — replaced with the real US-100mg-vs-EFSA-12mg comparison and the supplement-stacking risk; separate the tagline's food message from the nausea-treatment message, since "ordinary food, and real help if nausea" could read as food itself treating nausea; remove boiled-egg-alongside from all six breakfasts, the exact repetition pattern already corrected in Weeks 30 and 31; reduce banana from roughly seven appearances to three; vary the three fish preparations, which had all been the same curry renamed; fix two dinner B options that had dropped a vegetable component or paired chicken tikka with plain rice) → v2 9.3/10 ("one very small v3, then lock" — 3 final meal-structure corrections: Day 3 breakfast B's "whichever gentle protein you can tolerate" was too vague to actually select, replaced with a concrete "plain toast with a fully cooked scrambled egg, if tolerated, and a banana"; Day 3's nourishment break had lost its A/B structure entirely, restored to two real selectable options (plain crackers/dry toast vs. curd with toast, if tolerated) while keeping the compassionate "no need to force a particular option" line; Day 6's full all-B path had chicken at all three main meals — breakfast sandwich, lunch curry, dinner stew — fixed by changing lunch B to a fully cooked egg curry; plus an optional precision improvement, applied directly: naming both the adult (100mg, 19+) and pregnant-adolescent (80mg, 14-18) US upper limits rather than only the adult figure) → locked directly off the "After the three meal corrections, Week 32 is ready to lock" verdict, per the standing rule. Saved as `Nourish - Third Trimester Week 32 (LOCKED).docx`. Week 32's own "Looking ahead" line names **copper** as next — Claude's best guess, not yet verified against the full locked-week theme list; confirm before treating it as settled, per the standing rule below.

**Week 32 lessons, worth carrying into every future week:** (1) a "morning sickness" or similarly-themed day must be actively re-adapted to the mother's actual current week, not drafted from a generic first-trimester template and left as-is — late-pregnancy nausea has a genuinely different differential (pre-eclampsia, dehydration) that a Week-1-style "try vitamin B6" framing completely omits; check what week the content is landing in and whether the standard framing for that symptom still applies there; (2) a real, professionally-issued dosing schedule (e.g. ACOG's 10-25mg 3-4x/day) should not be transcribed into mother-facing text as if it were a self-directed instruction, even when accurately sourced — when the total approaches or exceeds a separate safety limit (here, EFSA's 12mg/day UL), the safe move is to direct her to her care team for the specific product/dose rather than hand her the numbers to use herself; (3) a toxicity-reassurance sentence can be accurate in isolation and still misleading in effect — "very high doses over months to years" is technically true but undersells why a health authority (EFSA) set its limit nearly 10x lower than another (the US); when two authorities diverge this widely, the wording needs to reflect the divergence itself, not just the more permissive framing; (4) a tagline or mantra combining a food message and a treatment/symptom message in one sentence risks implying the food treats the symptom — keep them as two distinct statements even when they're thematically related; (5) the "no boiled-egg-alongside on every breakfast" correction from Weeks 30-31 needs to be checked proactively at draft time for every new week, not just caught reactively in review — it recurred again in Week 32's own v1 despite being flagged as a standing pattern twice already; (6) a supportive-sounding line like "whichever protein you can tolerate" is not a valid A/B option — every choice in the meal chart must remain concretely selectable, even on a day framed around flexibility and low pressure; compassion and structure aren't in tension, both are achievable together; (7) auditing a day's full all-B path for protein-category repetition must be done even on a day framed as "away from home"/practical, where it's tempting to assume convenience food defaults (chicken sandwich, chicken curry, chicken stew) won't repeat — they can, and did.

**Week 33 (copper) — LOCKED and LIVE, 2026-08-05, same session as Week 32.** Topic verified clear of duplication against the full list of 32 locked-week themes before drafting, with one honest caveat logged and worth carrying forward: **Weeks 2-8's locked `.docx` files were not present in this session's mounted `mom-village` folder** (only a stray "Week 2 (DRAFT v1)" file exists) — only Weeks 1 and 9-32 (26 files) could be pandoc-converted and directly checked this session. Copper does not appear as a theme in any of those 26, and appears only once in passing (inside Week 21's zinc-supplement interaction caution), never as a featured topic. CLAUDE.md's own history documents Weeks 2-8 as locked and confirmed, so this was treated as a file-visibility gap rather than reason to doubt the lock — but it means the duplicate check for Weeks 2-8 specifically relied on this file's documented history, not a fresh reconversion. **Worth a real check in a future session: confirm Weeks 2-8's locked files still exist in the actual `/Users/roop/Downloads/mom-village/` folder Roop sees (as opposed to this sandbox's mount of it), and if they're genuinely missing, that's worth flagging to Roop directly rather than assuming it's just a session-mount quirk.** ICMR-NIN 2020's full RDA/EAR report (re-fetched this session) confirms copper has no pregnancy-specific figure — general adult only, 1.7 mg/day, sitting in the same "other minerals and trace elements" table as selenium/potassium/manganese/chromium, same gap pattern as vitamin E and vitamin K. NIH ODS's Copper fact sheet gives the pregnancy RDA (1,000 mcg/day) and ULs (10,000 mcg/day adults 19+/pregnancy, 8,000 mcg/day pregnant adolescents 14-18) used as the labeled international reference. Went through two review rounds: v1 7.8/10 (8 corrections: fix Day 5's full all-B path containing two egg moments — breakfast omelette and lunch egg curry — by changing lunch B to a chicken-and-sabut-moong curry, which also kept the copper-relevant legume; audit every non-vegetarian B option for silently dropping the week's plant copper source rather than retaining one alongside the animal protein — fixed on Days 1, 2, 3, and 5 with chana, sesame tempering, spinach, and garnishes; correct an overly narrow "old copper pipes" framing for excess-exposure risk, replaced with the fuller NIH-sourced picture naming high-dose supplements, general plumbing/water chemistry, and Wilson's disease; soften Day 3's fetal-copper-storage language so it doesn't imply the mother must actively increase her intake to build the baby's stores; reduce a repeated cashew-plus-"a glass of milk" pattern that had become the default way A became B across five spots; replace an unnatural "loose sesame seeds with jaggery" snack with real named dishes (til-gur chikki, til laddu); replace Day 1's chicken cutlet — not itself a meaningful copper choice — with chicken-and-chana curry; verify manganese against the locked-week theme list before naming it as next week's teaser rather than publishing an admitted guess) → v2 9.2/10 (3 final corrections: Day 6's nourishment break still had milk at both breakfast AND the nourishment break despite the v2 changelog claiming this pattern was removed — the actual B option was "the same as A, plus a glass of milk" with an extra internal "or" choice — replaced with two genuinely distinct, portable options; the manganese-verification internal note overstated certainty by claiming duplication was "confirmed clear" when only 26 of 32 locked-week files could actually be checked directly — corrected to name the specific gap plainly and flagged for confirmation while drafting Week 34; Day 7's reflection reintroduced the pressure removed from Day 3 with "why these final weeks matter specifically for your baby's own copper stores," which could again imply her eating determines the outcome — reworded to focus on what copper does rather than what the mother must ensure; plus an optional meal-polish fix, applied directly: Day 5 dinner B's "fish-and-vegetable stir-fry, such as sardines" swapped for the more natural "lower-mercury fish curry or pan-cooked sardines, served with rice and a sesame-tempered vegetable stir-fry") → locked directly off the "revise the three points above, then Week 33 can be locked" verdict, per the standing rule. Saved as `Nourish - Third Trimester Week 33 (LOCKED).docx`. Week 33's own "Looking ahead" line names **manganese** as next — checked against the 26 directly-checkable locked themes this session (clear), but per the caveat above, this should be given one more real check against Weeks 2-8 specifically while drafting Week 34, not just assumed settled.

**Week 33 lessons, worth carrying into every future week:** (1) when a nutrient's own honest framing says it "leans toward plant foods," every non-vegetarian B option needs an active audit for whether it silently drops that plant source entirely rather than keeping one alongside the animal protein — the non-veg path becoming less aligned with the week's own stated nutritional story is a distinct bug from the more familiar "B drops the featured food" pattern, worth checking as its own step; (2) a safety-risk sentence naming one specific narrow cause (e.g. "old pipes") can be accurate as a description of one pathway while still being an incomplete, potentially misleading picture of the real risk landscape (supplements, general plumbing/water chemistry, a diagnosed condition) — check whether a safety explanation names the full, real category of causes rather than the single most vivid example; (3) a "why this matters now" or biological-timing explanation can still smuggle in mother-responsibility framing even after an explicit correction pass — the same pressure that was removed from one section (Day 3) can resurface almost verbatim in a later section (Day 7's reflection) discussing the same biological fact, so a "does this again imply she must actively increase/ensure X" check needs to be applied to every section referencing that fact, not just the section where it was first corrected; (4) an internal verification-note claim about what was "removed" or "reduced" must be checked with the same literal re-reading discipline whether it's about counting occurrences (established Weeks 28-31) or about a structural pattern (here: "B is no longer just A plus milk") — the Day 6 nourishment break in v2 still had the milk-added structure at both breakfast and the snack despite the changelog explicitly claiming this exact pattern had been fixed; (5) a duplicate-check claim should state its own actual scope honestly — "checked against the theme list" is a different, weaker claim than "checked against all locked files," and when some files are genuinely inaccessible (as Weeks 2-8 were this session), the internal note needs to say so plainly rather than round up to full confidence; (6) offering a mother a choice with an embedded secondary "or" inside one option (e.g. "an apple with peanut butter, or roasted chana") adds real decision friction that the app's whole A/B structure exists to avoid — each option should resolve to one clear, picturable choice, not a nested decision.

**Week 34 (manganese) — LOCKED and LIVE, 2026-08-05, same session as Week 33. A real trimester-independent food-safety contradiction was caught and fixed.** Topic verified clear of duplication against the full list of 33 locked-week themes, with the same honest Weeks-2-8 caveat as Week 33 (re-checked this session — those locked files are still not present in this session's mounted folder; flagged directly to Roop in-chat, not just logged here). ICMR-NIN 2020's full RDA/EAR report confirms manganese has no pregnancy-specific figure — general adult only, 4 mg/day, in the same "other minerals and trace elements" table as copper/selenium/potassium/chromium. NIH ODS's Manganese fact sheet was handled with particular terminology care: manganese has no RDA at all (the Food and Nutrition Board found the evidence insufficient to set one), only an **Adequate Intake (AI)** — 2.0 mg/day in pregnancy — verified precisely against the source table's own header rather than assumed, per the standing lesson from Week 32. UL: 11 mg/day adults 19+/pregnancy, 9 mg/day pregnant adolescents 14-18.

Went through two review rounds: v1 7.6/10 ("scientifically promising, but not yet safe or varied enough" — the most urgent issue was a genuine mother-facing food-safety contradiction: Day 2 offered a raw sprouted-moong salad while this project's own fixed food-safety footer explicitly says to cook sprouts and never eat them raw; 9 further corrections: soften "plant-exclusive" to "plant-forward" with a genuine shellfish exception (mussels/oysters/clams are real manganese sources) since blanket exclusivity overstated the case; remove a manufactured clinical justification from Day 3's iron-manganese connection ("this is one more reason getting your iron checked" implied manganese absorption itself was a reason to test iron, when it isn't); stop presenting manganese and vitamin K as a demonstrated partnership — both support clotting through separate, non-interchangeable roles; reduce heavy repetition of pineapple (3 days), peanuts (5 days), and brown rice (4 days) — the exact "signature ingredient becomes the whole week's identity" pattern flagged in earlier weeks; fix a near-duplicate Day 3/Day 4 dinner (both "roti with mixed vegetable(-and-chicken) curry") by changing Day 4 to whole-wheat noodles; remove a returned "A plus a glass of milk" pattern on Day 1 and Day 6; add explicit refrigeration language to overnight oats; replace an unnatural "egg mixed into idli batter" with a more familiar idli-and-bhurji pairing; pull back a chromium "next week" teaser that was still an admitted unverified guess) → v2 9.4/10 (2 final refinements: Day 7's reflection had reintroduced the manganese-vitamin-K "connection" language Day 3 had just carefully separated — reworded to reference manganese's own roles in metabolism/bone/clotting without implying a direct relationship to iron or vitamin K specifically; two remaining "same base food plus one addition" snack pairs (Day 4: pear/apple vs. pear/apple-plus-curd; Day 5: curd-fruit-flaxseed vs. curd-pomegranate-flaxseed) replaced with genuinely different foods and formats) → locked directly off the "Apply those two refinements and lock Week 34" verdict, per the standing rule. Saved as `Nourish - Third Trimester Week 34 (LOCKED).docx`. Week 34's own "Looking ahead" line was deliberately left generic ("another trace nutrient found in everyday foods") rather than naming chromium, since chromium could only be checked against 26 of the full 33 locked-week set this session — **name and verify Week 35's actual topic fresh at the start of that session**, don't assume chromium from this week's vague teaser.

**Week 34 lessons, worth carrying into every future week:** (1) the fixed, universal food-safety footer (raw sprouts, pasteurized dairy, etc.) is a real constraint on every week's meal content, not just boilerplate to append at the end — a week's own new meal ideas (like a "healthy" raw-sprout salad) must be checked against that footer's specific rules before drafting, not just against general food-safety instinct; (2) a "this nutrient comes mainly from plant foods" claim needs the same precision as a numeric one — "exclusive" is a stronger, different claim than "forward" or "mainly," and a genuine minority exception (shellfish, here) should be named rather than rounded away for a cleaner-sounding sentence; (3) a real, citable biological interaction (iron status affecting manganese absorption) can still be misused if the copy leans on it to manufacture a *new* reason for a clinical action (iron testing) that the interaction itself doesn't establish — the actual reason to test iron is the standard pregnancy-anemia rationale already covered elsewhere, and the copy shouldn't imply this week's nutrient adds a fresh justification; (4) two nutrients sharing a broad physiological category (both "support clotting," both "support bone") doesn't mean they interact with each other — describe each nutrient's own role and explicitly say they aren't interchangeable rather than implying a partnership or connection between them; (5) a repetition fix applied to one section (Day 3's science) doesn't automatically propagate to a later section restating the same idea in different words (Day 7's reflection) — every section touching a corrected claim needs its own re-check, the same lesson as Week 33's Day 7 miss, now confirmed as a recurring failure mode worth watching every week; (6) reducing repetition of a "signature" week ingredient (pineapple, peanuts, brown rice here) needs to happen before v1 is presented, not just after review catches it — this project's own research/drafting step should include a self-audit pass counting how many days lean on the same 2-3 headline foods; (7) a near-duplicate meal across adjacent days within the SAME week (not just across weeks) is worth checking too — Day 3 and Day 4's dinners were nearly identical despite being in the same draft, a repetition check that's easy to miss when focused only on cross-week duplication; (8) when a "looking ahead" topic can only be partially verified (missing Weeks 2-8 again), don't publish it by name even as a guess — use a genuinely generic placeholder line rather than naming an unverified topic in mother-facing copy, and treat the next session's topic selection as needing a fresh check, not inherited from this week's placeholder.

**Week 35 (thiamine / vitamin B1) — LOCKED and LIVE, 2026-08-05, same session as Week 34. A candidate topic was researched and deliberately rejected before drafting began — worth reusing this judgment call in future weeks.** Chromium was the first candidate considered (grouped with copper/manganese/selenium in ICMR-NIN's own trace-element table, all already covered) and confirmed clear of duplication, but research turned up a genuine complication none of this series' prior "no ICMR pregnancy figure" nutrients (vitamin E, vitamin K, copper, manganese) had: chromium's status as an essential nutrient at all is now disputed in more recent science. NIH's own Chromium Health Professional Fact Sheet cites a 2017 paper titled "New evidence against chromium as an essential trace element," states chromium deficiency has never been demonstrated in a healthy population, and notes recent chromium-supplementation trials for diabetes/PCOS/weight loss show only small, clinically uncertain effects. Building a week around "make sure you get enough" of a nutrient whose essentiality itself is contested would overstate the science — so chromium was set aside and flagged to Roop transparently in the draft's own verification note, the same transparent-substitution pattern established at Week 25 (iodine replacing an unconfirmed calcium guess). Thiamine was chosen instead: verified via grep against all 26 directly-checkable locked-week files (same Weeks-2-8 file-visibility caveat as every recent week) that it had never been a week theme. Unlike several recent weeks, thiamine has a genuine, confirmed ICMR-NIN 2020 pregnancy-specific RDA sitting directly in the primary RDA/EAR tables — 2.0 mg/day (EAR 1.6 mg/day) — versus NIH's US pregnancy RDA of 1.4 mg/day, a real difference presented honestly as two reference systems rather than picking one. No UL exists (thiamine is water-soluble and excess is excreted; NIH states harmful effects from usual intake haven't been reported, evidence is just limited — a distinct, weaker claim than "proven safe at any dose"). India's rice-fortification claim was independently verified rather than assumed: FSSAI's mandatory fortified-rice premix is iron + folic acid + vitamin B12 only — thiamine is merely an optional addition some schemes choose to include, so the content correctly says not to count on it. The week's one real safety inclusion — severe/prolonged vomiting (hyperemesis gravidarum) can deplete thiamine seriously enough to cause Wernicke's encephalopathy — was independently verified via multiple case reports/reviews and, on review, against ACOG's nausea-and-vomiting-of-pregnancy practice bulletin and RCOG's Green-top Guideline 69, both of which support thiamine as part of clinical management of prolonged vomiting.

Went through two review rounds: v1 7.3/10 ("strong topic and research, but v2 needs nutritional-language and meal-variety revisions" — 10 corrections: simplify the RDA-comparison opening, which read as defensive ("genuine, confirmed," "real step up," "meaningfully lower") and invited the mother to wonder which figure she had to meet, replaced with neutral wording presenting both figures as background, not a target; correct the no-upper-limit explanation, since "the body excretes what it doesn't need" is too certain a claim — the accurate version is that no UL exists because harm from usual intake hasn't been reported and evidence is limited, not that any dose is proven safe; calm and shorten the hyperemesis-gravidarum day, which had grown too long, named Wernicke's encephalopathy explicitly, and included clinician-facing detail about IV-dextrose sequencing — replaced with a plain instruction to contact the maternity team if vomiting is severe or dehydration signs appear; cut sunflower seeds from six consecutive days down to a rotation across chana, chaat, crackers, flaxseed, peanuts, and trail mix; remove a Day 1 "same snack plus milk" A/B pair; diversify dinners that had converged on the same "roti + mixed vegetable curry + dal" shape into five genuinely different formats (khichdi, pasta, pulao, curry, soup); remove brown rice from two of three consecutive-day occurrences so it appears only once across the week; rebuild Day 6 entirely, which had become four consecutive bread-or-handheld meals with curd repeated at both lunch and dinner; replace a reflection question that assumed every household drains and discards dal-cooking water, a household-practice assumption not everyone shares; shorten an overly institutional rice-fortification explanation naming ration shops/anganwadis/school schemes down to two plain sentences) → v2 9.4/10 (2 final corrections: Day 1's "a small handful of ground nuts" is ambiguous in Indian English — could read as groundnuts/peanuts or as nuts that have been ground — replaced with "one tablespoon of finely ground mixed nuts"; Day 6 breakfast B had egg replacing peanut butter entirely rather than sitting alongside a real thiamine source, fixed by keeping a portion of roasted chana in the B option alongside the egg; plus an optional realism polish, applied directly: Day 1's "vegetable uttapam" swapped for the more recognisable "vegetable dal-and-rice dosa," since Day 3 already used a besan chilla and two similar pancake-style breakfasts in one week read as repetitive) → locked directly off the "Make the two small corrections above and lock Week 35" verdict, per the standing rule. Saved as `Nourish - Third Trimester Week 35 (LOCKED).docx`. Week 35's own "Looking ahead" line is deliberately left generic ("another everyday nutrient your body relies on for steady energy and function"), following the same precedent Week 34 set — no specific Week 36 topic is named or implied.

**Week 35 lessons, worth carrying into every future week:** (1) a nutrient's own real evidence base can disqualify it as a candidate topic entirely, not just shape how it's framed — when a nutrient's essentiality itself is disputed in current science (chromium, here), forcing it into this series' standard "make sure you get enough" template would misrepresent the science; check not just "has this been covered" and "does ICMR-NIN have a figure" but "is this nutrient's basic essential status actually settled" before committing to draft it; (2) presenting two authorities' differing figures side by side needs genuinely neutral language — words like "meaningfully lower" or "a real step up," even when factually accurate, can read as inviting the mother to decide which number she's failing to meet; state both plainly as different reference systems and stop there; (3) "no upper limit has been established" and "unlimited amounts are safe" are two different claims — the honest version is that harm from ordinary intake hasn't been reported and evidence is limited, not that safety at any dose has been proven; this distinction is likely to recur for other water-soluble vitamins later in this series; (4) a real, clinically-serious safety fact (thiamine deficiency causing Wernicke's encephalopathy after severe vomiting) can still be over-delivered — naming the specific rare diagnosis and including clinician-sequencing detail (thiamine before IV dextrose) is more than a mother needs; the safe, actionable version is simply "contact your care team if vomiting is severe or you show dehydration signs," trusting the clinical team to handle the specifics; (5) a single ingredient repeated across most of a week's active days (sunflower seeds on six of seven days here) is exactly the "signature ingredient" failure mode flagged in Week 34 and earlier weeks, and needs a proactive count during drafting, not just a reactive fix after review catches it; (6) "vary the dal" is not the same fix as "vary the meal," a distinction sharper here than in earlier weeks — five days built around the identical "roti + mixed vegetable curry + a small bowl of dal" shape, just with a different pulse each time, still reads as the same meal to a mother working through the week; vary the dish format itself (khichdi, pulao, pasta, soup, curry), not only the named legume inside it; (7) an "away from home" day built entirely from bread-based, handheld foods (toast, roll, sandwich, toast again) is its own repetition failure distinct from ingredient repetition — check meal *format* diversity within a single day, not just across the week; (8) when a non-vegetarian B option swaps in an egg, confirm the week's actual target nutrient's food source is retained alongside it, not silently dropped — egg contributes very little thiamine, so a B path built around egg-replacing-peanut-butter would have quietly stopped being about this week's nutrient at all, the same ingredient-level A/B parity discipline established at Week 33 now recurring as a fresh instance to watch for on every future week; (9) an ambiguous food-naming term in Indian English (a "handful" of "ground nuts" reads differently than intended) is worth a plain-language double-check the same way species names and technical terms already get checked — quantity phrasing needs to make physical sense for the actual prepared form of the food, not just for its whole form.

**Week 36 (riboflavin / vitamin B2) — LOCKED and LIVE, 2026-08-05, same session as Week 35.** Topic verified clear of duplication against all 28 directly-checkable locked-week files (Weeks 1, 9-35, freshly re-pandoc-converted this session) — riboflavin had never appeared, even in passing. ICMR-NIN 2020's full RDA/EAR report (re-fetched fresh, not assumed from prior-session memory) gives riboflavin a genuine, confirmed pregnancy-specific figure sitting directly in the primary RDA/EAR tables — RDA 2.7 mg/day, EAR 2.3 mg/day — versus 1.9 mg/day RDA for non-pregnant adult women. NIH ODS's Riboflavin Health Professional Fact Sheet confirms its own US pregnancy RDA as 1.4 mg/day. No UL exists (same "evidence is limited, not proven safe at any dose" framing as Week 35's thiamine). Liver is riboflavin's single most concentrated food source but was deliberately excluded from the meal chart itself (only mentioned educationally) since Week 28 already established pregnancy should limit liver for its vitamin A content — cross-referencing an earlier week's standing caution rather than silently contradicting it.

Went through three review rounds — the most rounds any week has needed since the reviewer raised a new dimension each time rather than repeating the same issue: v1 6.9/10 ("good nutrient choice, but not ready to lock" — 10 corrections: correct a cooking-mechanism claim, since riboflavin's real cooking loss is water-solubility/discarded cooking water, not generic "heat," matching NIH's actual wording; rebuild Day 6 entirely, since "milk, curd, and eggs travel easily" ignored real cold-chain risk — perishable dairy/egg/chicken items need refrigeration or an insulated cold pack, not casual travel; reduce dairy from three-to-four occasions on some days down to one or two, since the plan read as a "dairy prescription" unsuitable for mothers who are lactose intolerant, vegan, or dislike milk; add a genuine non-dairy note (mushrooms, spinach, almonds, fortified foods) rather than implying dairy is necessary; replace a deficiency-symptom day that was too reassuring ("usually improves quickly") given how nonspecific mouth/tongue/eye symptoms are, with calmer, more inclusive framing that explicitly discourages self-diagnosis; reframe a preeclampsia day whose closing line ("one more reason ordinary food sources matter") still implied food choices lower risk, when supplementation evidence is genuinely mixed; ensure every non-vegetarian option keeps a real riboflavin source (spinach, mushroom, almond, curd) alongside chicken rather than chicken silently replacing one; reduce paneer from five occurrences to two; fix a curd-rice-with-pickle side that added salt without nutritional relevance to the theme, plus an internal editing error claiming "the same cooked vegetable sabzi" on days where the A option didn't actually have one; make the closing reflection inclusive of non-dairy routes) → v2 8.7/10 ("one focused revision round before locking" — 5 corrections: break an almond-or-peanut snack loop that had simply replaced the earlier dairy loop, appearing on all six active days, with six genuinely varied nourishment breaks (mushroom-corn chaat, spinach-corn sandwich, fortified cereal, mushroom toast, roasted-chana trail mix); make the non-dairy route actually usable inside the meal chart itself rather than just described in the introduction — several breakfasts still had no genuinely dairy-free A/B option, fixed by adding an explicit "wherever milk is listed, choose a label-verified fortified soy drink" substitution instruction and rebuilding the specific breakfasts/lunch named; fix Day 1's dinner B, which paired chicken with an unspecified vegetable rather than a real riboflavin source — added mushroom and spinach explicitly; soften an overly broad "fish carries comparatively little riboflavin" claim to acknowledge real variation across fish/seafood species; condense a preeclampsia paragraph that repeated "antenatal checks matter" twice) → locked directly off the "Revise these five points, then Week 36 should be ready to lock" verdict, per the standing rule. Saved as `Nourish - Third Trimester Week 36 (LOCKED).docx`. Week 36's own "Looking ahead" line is deliberately left generic, following the same precedent Weeks 34 and 35 set — no specific Week 37 topic is named or implied.

**Week 36 lessons, worth carrying into every future week:** (1) fixing one repetition problem (dairy overuse) can silently create a new one (almond/peanut overuse) if the fix is applied narrowly to "remove X" rather than "ensure genuine variety across the whole category" — after any repetition fix, re-scan the *replacement* ingredients across all seven days for the same signature-ingredient pattern, don't assume a fix is complete just because the flagged ingredient count went down; (2) a "you can substitute Y" note placed only in the document's introduction is not the same as a usable path through the actual meal chart — if a mother is meant to have a genuinely dairy-free (or any other) route, every relevant meal slot needs its own real A/B option reflecting that, not just a general disclaimer at the top; (3) a broad claim about an entire food category ("fish has little riboflavin") needs the same species-level caution this project already applies to mercury safety — real variation within a category can make a general statement technically wrong even when the overall direction is correct; (4) when a nutrient's real safety story is genuinely about food-safety/cold-chain risk (as opposed to a nutrient-specific danger), don't understate it with a casual line like "X travels easily" — perishable protein/dairy items have real, well-defined safe-handling limits (FDA: 2 hours at room temperature, 1 hour above ~32°C) that deserve the same explicit treatment as any other safety fact in this series; (5) auditing "every non-vegetarian option retains a real target-nutrient source" must be re-checked after every meal-variety edit, not just once at draft time — Day 1's dinner B lost its retained source (mushroom/spinach) during a mid-revision swap and needed a second, separate fix in the next round; (6) a genuinely uncertain nutrient-outcome link (riboflavin and preeclampsia here, similar to magnesium/leg cramps and potassium/blood pressure earlier in this series) can still leak an implied causal claim through a single closing sentence even after the main paragraph is properly hedged — re-read the *last line* of any uncertain-evidence day specifically, since that's where an overreaching implication tends to hide; (7) this was the first week in the series to need three full review rounds rather than the usual two, because each round surfaced a genuinely new class of issue rather than the same one recurring — worth remembering that "one more round" isn't itself a red flag if the corrections are substantively different each time, only if the *same* issue keeps resurfacing uncorrected.

**Week 37 (niacin / vitamin B3) — LOCKED and LIVE, 2026-08-05, same session as Week 36.** Topic verified clear of duplication against all 29 directly-checkable locked-week files (Weeks 1, 9-36) via fresh grep for niacin/nicotinamide/vitamin B3/pantothenic/biotin/phosphorus — all clear. ICMR-NIN 2020's full RDA/EAR report gives niacin's general-adult figure for a sedentary woman as EAR 9 mg/day, RDA 11 mg/day (quoted directly from the report's own prose), with pregnancy shown in the RDA/EAR summary table as a +2 mg/day increment rather than a standalone absolute figure like thiamine or riboflavin had — presented honestly as an increment (11 + 2 = ~13 mg/day) rather than restated as a flat ICMR pregnancy RDA. NIH ODS's Niacin Health Professional Fact Sheet (freshly fetched): US pregnancy RDA 18 mg NE/day; a genuine, age-specific Tolerable Upper Intake Level exists for niacin — unlike thiamine/riboflavin's "no UL established" — 30 mg/day for ages 14-18 and 35 mg/day for ages 19+, applying only to supplemental/fortified/medicinal niacin (flushing from nicotinic-acid supplements starts around 30 mg; no adverse effects reported from food niacin). A genuinely useful, verified differentiator: eggs contain almost no niacin directly (0 mg per large egg in NIH's own food-source table), so the meal chart deliberately uses chicken, fish, legumes, whole grains, and seeds as the real niacin swaps rather than defaulting to egg as several earlier weeks did. No condition-specific note included — the one candidate link (high-dose supplemental nicotinic acid affecting blood glucose) applies only at pharmacological doses (1.5g+/day) far beyond any dietary or prenatal-supplement level, so a diabetes_gd note would have been unverified-link overreach.

Went through two review rounds: v1 6.8/10 ("would not lock this version yet... the meal design has slipped back into the repetition problem we originally set out to solve" — 7 corrections: fix a genuinely false internal verification claim ("no day allows three chicken moments") by actually rebuilding the meals, not just deleting the note — v1's all-B path had stacked chicken across breakfast, lunch, and dinner on four separate days; cut peanut/peanut-butter appearances from roughly ten occurrences across the week down to a small number, rotating instead across roasted chana, sunflower/pumpkin seeds, whole grains, brown rice, green peas, legumes, mushrooms, potatoes, and fortified cereal; replace several chicken options with one lower-mercury fish meal, egg options, or a second vegetarian preparation, and clarify that when egg is offered, the accompanying grain/legume/seed provides the more meaningful direct niacin contribution while egg mainly adds protein/tryptophan; fix Day 6's food-safety framing — "shelf-stable milk" was inaccurate once opened/unrefrigerated, the cold-chain instruction omitted the hard-boiled egg from breakfast, and lunch (frankie) and dinner (wrap) were functionally the same meal; correct the supplement upper-limit figures, which had incorrectly given a single 35mg figure for both pregnancy age bands rather than the real age-specific values (30mg for 14-18, 35mg for 19+); rework the Day 7 reflection and "today" text, which implied this series had wrongly relied on eggs as a universal nutrient replacement, in a way that could undermine confidence in the programme; remove the internal verification/changelog block at lock time as always, but first make it factually accurate rather than just deleting the false claim) → v2 9.4/10 ("major improvement... almost ready to lock" — one required correction: the intro's "If you eat meat, fish, or eggs" callout claimed chicken, turkey, and fish were "each used at different points," but turkey never appeared anywhere in the actual meal chart — fixed by removing turkey from both the callout and the Day 2 teaching paragraph, since an unused named ingredient in mother-facing text is its own small but real accuracy issue) → locked directly off the "Once the turkey sentence and internal material are removed, I am comfortable marking Week 37 as locked" verdict, per the standing rule. Saved as `Nourish - Third Trimester Week 37 (LOCKED).docx`. Week 37's "Looking ahead" line is deliberately left generic, continuing the precedent set at Weeks 34-36 — no specific Week 38 topic is named or implied.

**Week 37 lessons, worth carrying into every future week:** (1) when a review flags a repetition problem, the fix must happen in the actual meals, not just in the internal verification note describing them — v1's changelog claimed "no day allows three chicken moments" while the real meal chart had exactly that on four separate days; an internal claim about what the meals do must be checked against a fresh trace of the real content, not asserted from intent; (2) cutting an overused ingredient by "at least half" can still be over-corrected to near-zero, which is its own small loss (a genuinely good, verified food source disappearing from the plan entirely) — when a reviewer asks for a cut, aim to retain the ingredient at a minority share rather than eliminating it outright, unless there's a separate reason (safety, repetition of a *different* ingredient) to remove it completely; (3) fixing one animal-protein's overuse (chicken) by introducing egg more often can recreate the exact "egg silently fails to carry the week's target nutrient" bug from earlier weeks unless every egg option explicitly says what nearby food is doing the real nutritional work — this week's callout and every egg-paired meal both state plainly that the accompanying grain/legume/seed, not the egg, carries the niacin; (4) a "same format, different protein" pair (this week's Day 6 lunch-frankie/dinner-wrap) is a repetition failure even when the two items have different names — check meal *format* across a single day's full menu, not just ingredient overlap, the same lesson Week 35 first surfaced for an away-from-home day; (5) when two authorities set an age-banded rather than single safety limit (here, the US niacin UL genuinely differs by age: 30mg for 14-18 vs 35mg for 19+), presenting only one figure for both bands is a distinct, catchable error from the more familiar "picked the wrong authority" mistake — always check whether a cited limit is banded before collapsing it to one number; (6) correcting a self-critical or confidence-undermining tone (Week 37's Day 7 reflection implying the series had been "wrong" about eggs in earlier weeks) matters as much as correcting a factual error — mother-facing copy should teach a new distinction without casting doubt on previously-given, accurate guidance; (7) naming a food in a "why it matters" or introductory callout (turkey, here) creates an implicit claim that it appears in the meal chart — any food named in scene-setting copy needs a fresh check against the actual seven-day content before locking, since an unused named ingredient is a small but real, independently catchable accuracy gap, distinct from a wrong number or an overclaimed benefit.

**Week 38 (pantothenic acid / vitamin B5) — LOCKED and LIVE, 2026-08-05, same session as Week 37.** Topic verified clear of duplication against all 30 directly-checkable locked-week files (Weeks 1, 9-37) — pantothenic acid, biotin, phosphorus, molybdenum, chloride, and fluoride all confirmed via grep as never used; sodium appears only in passing mentions inside Weeks 9, 15, 26, and 27 (calcium, iodine, and potassium themes respectively), never as its own featured topic. ICMR-NIN 2020's full RDA/EAR report (re-fetched fresh): pantothenic acid has no RDA at all — only an Adequate Intake of 5 mg/day for both non-pregnant and pregnant women, with no pregnancy increment (a genuine methodological difference from thiamine/riboflavin/niacin, which all had confirmed RDAs). NIH ODS's Pantothenic Acid Health Professional Fact Sheet: also only an AI, not an RDA (evidence insufficient for an EAR) — 5 mg/day non-pregnant, 6 mg/day pregnancy, a modest increment unlike ICMR-NIN's flat figure. No UL exists from either body — NIH reports no toxicity even at high intakes, only mild diarrhea at multi-gram supplemental doses, a distinctly more permissive safety story than niacin's flushing-based UL the week before. A genuinely useful teaching point: pantothenic acid's name derives from the Greek for "from everywhere," since small amounts occur in nearly every food — and unlike niacin, egg IS a real contributor this week (0.7mg/large egg), a deliberate, explicit callback contrast to Week 37's egg-is-negligible finding. Liver excluded from the meal chart (educational mention only) per the standing Week 28 vitamin-A caution. No condition-specific note — the only genuine risk group (PKAN, a rare inherited neurodegenerative disorder) has no dietary-management angle relevant to an ordinary pregnancy meal chart.

Went through two review rounds: v1 6.7/10 ("not ready to lock... has replaced chicken repetition with curd, paneer and mushroom repetition" — 7 corrections: fix a false internal verification claim about chicken frequency by describing the real pattern (chicken once each on Days 2, 4, 6) rather than an invented daily rule; cut curd, which had appeared on every single day and up to three times in one day (Day 4: breakfast, snack, and dinner), down to a small number of natural pairings; reduce paneer from four scattered "add a side of paneer bhurji" appearances (including twice in one day) to a couple of genuinely distinct dishes; reduce mushroom from five days down to two or three, introducing soy, sweet potato, broccoli, and cauliflower as fresh rotation; rebuild every flagged "A plus curd" pair (Day 1 dinner, Day 2 dinner, Day 5 breakfast) into a genuinely different principal dish rather than a portion addition; correct an overstated cooking-loss claim — pantothenic acid is reasonably stable during ordinary home cooking, with water-soluble leaching into cooking liquid the real practical concern, distinct from the much larger losses NIH reports for industrial milling/processing, which shouldn't be conflated with home cooking; simplify an overly academic Day 7 reflection into warmer, plainer language) → v2 9.0/10 ("one short correction round before locking" — 3 required fixes: the v2 changelog still overclaimed that "no ingredient beyond egg repeats more than twice," when sunflower seeds, roasted chana, and soy all reasonably appear more often as snack-tier or rotation staples — corrected to describe the real, now-acceptable pattern (snack ingredients recur across several days while principal meals rotate widely) rather than asserting a stronger claim than the chart actually supports; remove a stray "curd" reference from Day 6's cold-chain sentence, since no Day 6 meal contains curd anymore, and reword so a freshly-prepared hot chicken wrap isn't implied to need chilling; strengthen Day 3's dinner B, which was still an "A plus mushroom" construction, into a genuinely separate dish (mushroom-and-green-pea curry); plus an optional polish, applied directly, smoothing an odd "stirred through" phrasing on Day 5's breakfast B into a plainer "with a small sunflower-seed garnish") → locked directly off the "After those corrections, I would be comfortable locking Week 38 without another structural rewrite" verdict, per the standing rule. Saved as `Nourish - Third Trimester Week 38 (LOCKED).docx`. Week 38's "Looking ahead" line is deliberately left generic, continuing the precedent set at Weeks 34-37 — no specific Week 39 topic is named or implied.

**Week 38 lessons, worth carrying into every future week:** (1) fixing one repeated-ingredient problem (chicken) can recreate the identical failure mode with different ingredients (curd, paneer, mushroom) if the fix is applied as "swap the flagged food for a different single food" rather than "genuinely diversify across many foods" — the same lesson Week 36 first surfaced with almonds replacing dairy, now confirmed as a recurring risk worth actively checking for on every revision round, not just assumed fixed once the originally-flagged ingredient's count drops; (2) an "A plus one ingredient" B option (same dish, add curd/paneer/mushroom) reads as a portion addition, not a real choice, even when the added food is genuinely relevant to the week's nutrient — a real alternative should change the principal dish or protein, not just append something to it; (3) when auditing repetition, distinguish principal-meal ingredients (dal, protein, main vegetable) from snack-tier staples (roasted chana, sunflower seeds, peanuts) — snack ingredients recurring across several days is normal and acceptable for this series, and an internal verification note claiming zero ingredient repeats anywhere is a stronger, checkable claim than the meals actually support, distinct from the more familiar "false claim asserting a fix that wasn't made" pattern seen in Weeks 30, 33, and 37; (4) a safety instruction naming a food that no longer appears in that day's meals (Day 6's stray curd cold-chain mention, copied forward from an earlier draft) is the same class of error as Week 37's unused-turkey mention — any food named in a safety or teaching note needs a fresh check against that day's actual final meals, every revision round, not just once; (5) a numeric claim about nutrient loss during cooking needs the same source-precision discipline as an RDA figure — NIH's cited 20-80% processing-loss range describes industrial milling and heavy processing broadly, not routine home cooking specifically, and conflating the two overstates an ordinary mother's real risk of losing the nutrient through normal meal preparation; (6) when a nutrient is genuinely reassuring on every axis (no RDA needed, no UL, deficiency essentially only in extreme conditions), the week's real narrative challenge shifts from teaching caution to keeping the meal chart itself interesting and varied — the science being simple doesn't make the meal-architecture discipline any less demanding, if anything it makes lazy repetition more tempting since there's no safety story to fill the space.

**Immediate next step for a new session:** recreate the build-script toolchain above, then determine Week 39's topic fresh — Week 36 deliberately left no specific "looking ahead" name to inherit, so this requires either resolving the Weeks 2-8 file-visibility gap first (checking with Roop directly whether those locked files exist in her real `/Users/roop/Downloads/mom-village/` folder) or proceeding with the same file-visibility caveat this session used, clearly flagged, against however many locked files are actually present. Before committing to any candidate nutrient, check not just whether it's already been covered and whether ICMR-NIN has a figure, but whether the nutrient's basic essential status is actually settled in current mainstream nutrition science (chromium was rejected at Week 35 for exactly this reason) — a quick check of NIH's own fact sheet language is usually enough to catch this. If checking an ICMR-NIN figure, check the full RDA/EAR report (not just the shorter brief_note.pdf) before concluding no figure exists — it contains prose-form RDA/EAR/AI statements for niacin, pantothenic acid, biotin, and folate/B12 (already covered) worth checking directly rather than re-deriving from memory. Remaining untried, never-covered nutrients confirmed via grep across Weeks 1, 9-38: biotin (B7, ICMR gives only an AI — 40µg/day for adults and pregnant women, no pregnancy increment noted in the summary text — verify precisely), phosphorus (has a real ICMR general-adult figure, 1000mg/day, no pregnancy split, 1:1 ratio with calcium per the report's own mineral section), sodium, molybdenum, chloride, fluoride. Niacin (B3) was used at Week 37, pantothenic acid (B5) at Week 38. Biotin is probably the strongest next candidate given it continues the B-vitamin thread and, like pantothenic acid, is AI-only rather than RDA-based — double-check the full RDA/EAR report's prose section directly rather than trusting this summary, and check biotin's essentiality/safety profile fresh (no reason to assume it mirrors pantothenic acid's very permissive story). Also confirm Week 37 is correctly labelled Third Trimester (see the confirmed boundary above). When auditing meal variety, check against at least the two immediately preceding locked weeks (Weeks 35 and 36), not just the single most recent one. After fixing any repetition problem, re-scan the replacement ingredients across all seven days for the same signature-ingredient pattern re-emerging (Week 36's almond/peanut loop replacing its own dairy loop is the cautionary example). If the week includes a genuine dietary-restriction accommodation (dairy-free, vegetarian, etc.), make sure the alternative is a real, selectable A/B option in the meal chart itself, not just a general note in the introduction. Audit every non-vegetarian option for retaining a real target-nutrient source after every revision round, not just the first draft. Re-read the closing sentence of any day discussing uncertain/mixed evidence specifically, since an overreaching implication tends to hide there even after the main paragraph is properly hedged. Before submitting any version for review, actually re-count/re-trace any claim made in the changelog against the real final document — this includes structural claims, not just numeric ones. When presenting two authorities' differing figures, use neutral "different reference system" language, not comparative words like "higher/lower/step up." Distinguish "no UL because harm hasn't been reported and evidence is limited" from "proven safe at any dose" for every future water-soluble-vitamin week. Do not re-litigate or re-open Weeks 1-36 — they are locked and confirmed; only revisit a locked week if Roop explicitly raises it.

**Catch-up entry, 2026-08-25 — this section had gone stale since the Week 38 update above and is now brought current.** The Trimester series (First/Second/Third Trimester, pregnancy weeks 1-40) continued past Week 38 and reached completion — all 40 weeks are locked and saved in `/Users/roop/Downloads/mom-village/` as `Nourish - [Trimester] Week N (LOCKED).docx`, confirmed directly against the folder listing. The detailed per-week review record for Weeks 39-40 predates what's available to the session performing this catch-up and isn't separately reconstructed here — treat Weeks 1-40 as locked and settled, and do not reopen them without Roop explicitly raising a specific week. Once the Trimester series finished, the project moved on to a second, separate numbered series covering the postpartum period — see "Postpartum series" immediately below. Note the file-naming split: Trimester-series files use `Nourish - [Trimester] Week N`, Postpartum-series files use `Nourish - Postpartum Week N` — the week numbers restart at 1 for postpartum and are NOT a continuation of the Trimester count.

**Postpartum series — what it is, and how it differs from the unrelated "postpartum" material documented later in this file.** This is the same Nourish docx-per-week project, continuing past the Trimester series into the postpartum period, with its own independent week count (Postpartum Week 1 = first week after birth).
